Kac diagrams for elliptic Weyl group elements
Stephen DeBacker, Jacob Haley

Abstract
. Suppose 𝔤 {\mathfrak{g}} is a semisimple complex Lie algebra and 𝔥 {\mathfrak{h}} is a Cartan subalgebra of 𝔤 {\mathfrak{g}} . To the pair ( 𝔤 , 𝔥 ) ({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{h}}) one can associate both a Weyl group and a set of Kac diagrams. There is a natural map from the set of elliptic conjugacy classes in the Weyl group to the set of Kac diagrams. In both this setting and the twisted setting, this paper (a) shows that this map is injective and (b) explicitly describes this map’s image.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 20G07; Secondary 20F55, 20E45

Introduction

Suppose
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a semisimple complex Lie algebra and
{\mathfrak{h}}
is a Cartan subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. To the pair
({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{h}})
one can associate both a Weyl group and a set of Kac diagrams. There is a natural map from the set of elliptic conjugacy classes in the Weyl group to the set of Kac diagrams. In both this setting and the twisted setting, this paper (a) shows that this map is injective and (b) explicitly describes this map’s image.

The map
\psi_{G}

Let
G
denote a connected semisimple complex group such that the Lie algebra of
G
is
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Let
A
denote the maximal torus in
G
such that the Lie algebra of
A
is
{\mathfrak{h}}
. Fix a pinning
(A,B,\{X_{\alpha}\,|\,\alpha\in\Delta\})
of
G
where
B
is a Borel subgroup of
G
that contains
A
,
\Delta
is the set of simple roots of
G
with respect to
A
and
B
, and
X_{\alpha}
is an element of the Lie algebra of the root group corresponding to
\alpha\in\Delta
. Fix
\vartheta\in\Aut({\mathfrak{g}})
that stabilizes this pinning. The automorphism
\vartheta
is called a pinned automorphism and has the following properties:
\vartheta(A)=A
,
\vartheta(B)=B
, and
\vartheta
permutes the elements of the set
\{X_{\alpha}\,|\,\alpha\in\Delta\}
. If
H
is a group on which
\vartheta
acts, then for
x,y\in H
we say that
x
is
\vartheta
-conjugate to
y
in
H
provided that there exists
h\in H
such that
x=hy\vartheta(h)^{-1}
.

Let
\tilde{W}
denote the Weyl group
N_{G}(A)/A
. Since
\tilde{W}
is generated by the simple reflections
w_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Delta
, we have that
\vartheta
acts on
\tilde{W}
.
An element
w\in\tilde{W}
is said to be
\vartheta
-elliptic provided that the
\vartheta
-conjugacy class of
w
in
\tilde{W}
does not intersect any proper subgroup of
\tilde{W}
of the form
\tilde{W}_{\theta}
for
\theta\subsetneq\Delta
with
\vartheta(\theta)=\theta
. Here
\tilde{W}_{\theta}
is the subgroup of
\tilde{W}
generated by the simple reflections
w_{\beta}
for
\beta\in\theta
.

Suppose
w_{1},w_{2}\in\tilde{W}
are
\vartheta
-elliptic. Let
n_{j}\in N_{G}(A)
be a lift of
w_{j}
.
Since all lifts of
w_{j}
into
N_{G}(A)
are
\vartheta
-conjugate by an element of
A
(see, for example,
[
1
, Lemma 1.1.3]
,
[
6
, Remark 4.1]
, or
[
8
, Theorem 1]
for the general case and
[
16
, Lemma 1.3]
or
[
23
, Section 4.1]
for the non-twisted case), we conclude that
w_{1}
is
\vartheta
-conjugate to
w_{2}
in
W
if and only if
n_{1}
is
\vartheta
-conjugate to
n_{2}
in
N_{G}(A)
. Moreover, both
n_{1}\rtimes\vartheta
and
n_{2}\rtimes\vartheta
have finite order in
N_{G}(A)\rtimes\langle\vartheta\rangle
. Thus, there exists a function
\psi_{G}
from
\tilde{W}^{\ee}_{\sim}
, the set of
\vartheta
-conjugacy classes of
\vartheta
-elliptic elements in
\tilde{W}
, to
G^{\vartheta-\tor}_{\sim}
, the set of
\vartheta
-conjugacy classes of
\vartheta
-torsion elements in
G
. An element
g\in G
is called a
\vartheta
-torsion element provided that
g\rtimes\vartheta
has finite order in
G\rtimes\langle\vartheta\rangle
.
In Section
3
we prove

Theorem
3.1.3
.

The map
\psi_{G}\,\colon\,\tilde{W}^{\ee}_{\sim}\rightarrow G^{\vartheta-\tor}_{\sim}
is injective.

Note that there is something to prove here. From the given we know that we have a well-defined map from
\tilde{W}^{\ee}_{\sim}
to the
\vartheta
-conjugacy classes of
\vartheta
-torsion elements in
N_{G}(A)
. To prove the theorem, one needs to show that if

n_{1},n_{2}\in N_{G}(A)
are lifts of
w_{1},w_{2}\in\tilde{W}^{\ee}
such that
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
are
\vartheta
-conjugate in
G
, then
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
are
\vartheta
-conjugate in
N_{G}(A)
; this is false if we don’t require both
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
to be
\vartheta
-elliptic.
The proof of Theorem
3.1.3
involves case-by-case checking.

Kac diagrams and the map
\chi_{G}

Reeder provides an absolutely beautiful treatment of Kac diagrams in
[
20
]
. Throughout this paper we have tried to align our treatment of Kac diagrams with the treatment found there.

Let
\Phi=\Phi(G,A)
denote the set of roots of
G
with respect to
A
. The perfect pairing
\langle\,,\,\rangle\,\colon\,\X_{*}(A)\times\X^{*}(A)
allows us to regard
\alpha\in\Phi
as a linear function on
V=\X_{*}(A)\otimes\mathbb{R}
.
If
S
denotes the maximal compact torus in
A
, then the map
\exp\,\colon\,V\rightarrow S
, defined by
\alpha(\exp(v))=e^{2\pi i\langle\alpha,v\rangle}
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
, is a surjective map. The hyperplanes
H_{\alpha,n}
in
V
defined by the kernels of the affine roots
v\mapsto\alpha(v)-n
for
\alpha\in\Phi
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
produce a simplicial decomposition of
V
, and the connected components in the complement of these hyperplanes are called alcoves. The affine Weyl group
\tilde{W}^{\aff}:=\X_{*}(A)\rtimes\tilde{W}
acts on
V
. This action preserves the hyperplane structure and
\tilde{W}^{\aff}
acts transitively on the alcoves. Our pinning
\{X_{\alpha}\,|\,\alpha\in\Delta\}
determines a unique alcove
C
that contains the origin of
V
in its closure. By uniqueness, we have
\vartheta(C)=C
.
Let
A^{\vartheta}
(resp.
G^{\vartheta}
) denote the connected component of the
\vartheta
-fixed points of
A
(resp.
G
).

Suppose
g
is a finite order element of
G
, say of order
m
. Fix a primitive
m^{\text{th}}
root of unity
\xi
. There exists
h\in G
such that
h^{-1}g\vartheta(h)
lives in the torus
A^{\vartheta}
(see, for example, Lemma
1.2.2
). Thus, there exists
\lambda\in\X_{*}(A^{\vartheta})
such that
h^{-1}g\vartheta(h)=\lambda(\xi)
.
After conjugating
\lambda(\xi)
by an element of the group
N_{G^{\vartheta}}(A)

we may assume that
\lambda/m
belongs to the closure of

C_{\vartheta}
, the alcove for
G^{\vartheta}
that contains the origin and intersects
C^{\vartheta}
nontrivially.
A Kac diagram associated to
g
encodes the location of
\lambda/m
via a labeling of the affine Dynkin diagram for
G^{\vartheta}
.
See Section
4
for the technical details of this labeling.

Let
\kappa
denote the set of Kac diagrams that arise as above, and we let
\kappa_{\sim}
denote the equivalence classes in
\kappa
determined by automorphisms of the affine Dynkin diagram of
G^{\vartheta}
arising from the action of
\Stab_{\tilde{W}^{\aff}_{\vartheta}}(C_{\vartheta})
. Here
\tilde{W}^{\aff}_{\vartheta}=(\proj_{V^{\vartheta}}\X_{*}(A))\rtimes\tilde{W}^{\vartheta}
is the affine Weyl group for
G^{\vartheta}
.
This association between
\vartheta
-torsion elements of
G
and Kac diagrams descends to a
map
\chi_{G}\,\colon\,G^{\vartheta-\tor}_{\sim}\rightarrow\kappa_{\sim}
. It is known (see, for example,
[
13
, Chapter X, Theorems 5.15 and 5.16]
,
[
14
, Chapter 8]
,
[
18
,
7^{\circ}
of Section 4 of Chapter 4]
, or
[
20
, Theorem 3.7]
) that this map is bijective when
G
is adjoint.

Under the assumption that
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple, in Section
4
we give a complete description of the Kac diagrams associated to
\vartheta
-elliptic elements in
\tilde{W}
.

The composition
\zeta_{G}=\chi_{G}\circ\psi_{G}
and applications

The composition
\zeta_{G}=\chi_{G}\circ\psi_{G}\,\colon\,\tilde{W}^{\ee}_{\sim}\rightarrow\kappa_{\sim}
has been extensively studied. For example, it is known to be independent of the isogeny type of
G
(see, for example,
[
1
, comments following Proposition 6.6]
or
[
5
, Remark 2.2.1, Lemma 3.3.6, and the fact that
x_{n}=x_{T_{n}}
encodes the Kac diagram associated to
n
]
), so we will write
\zeta
rather than
\zeta_{G}
. It is also known that every equivalence class in the image of
\zeta
is a singleton (see
[
1
, Proposition 6.8]
or
[
5
, Lemma 3.5.1]
). The map
\zeta
is known to be injective for groups of type
A_{n}
(there is only one elliptic conjugacy class in
\tilde{W}
) as well as groups of types

{{}^{2}\!}A_{2}
,

D_{m}
for
4\leq m\leq 8
,

{{}^{3}\!}D_{4}
,

E_{n}
,

{{}^{2}\!}E_{6}
,

F_{4}
, and

G_{2}

(see the tabulations in
[
1
]
,

[
3
]
,

[
16
]
,

[
21
]
, and
[
22
]
).
Moreover, if we denote by
\res_{\text{ell-reg}}\zeta
the restriction of
\zeta
to the set of regular (in the sense of Springer
[
26
]
)
\vartheta
-elliptic
\vartheta
-conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}
, then
\res_{\text{ell-reg}}\zeta
is known to be injective
[
19
, discussion in Section 2.1]
.

Since
\zeta
is independent of the isogeny type of
G
and
\chi_{G}
is a bijective map when
G
is adjoint, Theorem
3.1.3
implies

Corollary
2.1.1
.

The map
\zeta\,\colon\,\tilde{W}^{\ee}_{\sim}\rightarrow\kappa_{\sim}
is injective.

In Section
2
we show that Corollary
2.1.1
gives a new proof of the fact that every Weyl group is rational. In the context of totally ramified tame tori in a group over a field with non-trivial discrete valuation and cohomological dimension less than one, the Corollary implies that the point in the reduced building associated to such a torus completely determines the torus up to conjugation. We believe that this latter fact will play a role in harmonic analysis on reductive
p
-adic groups. Corollary
2.1.1
is also implicitly used in the proof of
[
1
, Proposition 7.1]
.

Remark
.

If we replace
\mathbb{C}
with any algebraically closed field, then the results of this paper still hold with very mild restrictions on
p
, the characteristic of the field. The theory of Kac diagrams goes through as long as one assumes that
p
does not divide the order of the
\vartheta
-torsion element under consideration
[
16
, Section 2]
. This restriction on
p
ensures that the roots of unity, denoted by
\xi
in this paper, that arise in the constructions exist.

Remark
.

Since applications will often require that
G
be reductive rather than semisimple, we note that if
G
is reductive, then one defines
\zeta
to be
\zeta_{G^{\prime}}
where
G^{\prime}
is the derived group of
G
.

Finally, this paper involves a fair bit of notational gymnastics. Although we independently derived our findings multiple times and compared our findings to results in the literature, there are bound to be typos and more serious errors. So, be cautious.

1.
Notation and two well-known results

1.1.
Notation

In addition to the notation introduced in the Introduction, we will need the following.

Let
K
denote the square matrix with ones on the anti-diagonal and zeroes elsewhere. The size of
K
will be evident from context. For example, if we are working with
G={\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}
, then
K
is a
(2\ell+1)\times(2\ell+1)
matrix.

If
X
is an
n\times m
matrix with entries in
\mathbb{C}
, then
X^{T}
denotes the transpose of
X
.

Suppose
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
\vec{\ell}=(\ell_{\mu}\geq\ell_{\mu-1}\geq\cdots\geq\ell_{1})
is a partition of
\ell
into
\mu
parts. For
1\leq\nu\leq\mu
define
\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}
:=
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\cdots+\ell_{\nu-1}
. Note that
\ell^{\prime}_{1}=0
and
\ell^{\prime}_{\mu}+\ell_{\mu}=\ell
.

For the vector space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
we let
e_{i}
denote the
i^{\text{th}}
standard basis vector for
1\leq i\leq n
. So
e_{1}=(1,0,0,\ldots,0)
,
e_{2}=(0,1,0,0,\ldots,0)
, etc.

Suppose
X
is set. If
\tau
is a function from
X
to
X
, then
X^{\tau}
denotes the set of
x\in X
such that
\tau(x)=x
. If
X
also has the structure of a connected complex group and

\tau
is an automorphism of
X
, then
X^{\tau}
denotes the
connected component
of the
\langle\tau\rangle
-fixed points of
X
.
If
y\in X
and
H
is a group that acts on
X
, then
{{}^{H}\!}y:=\{h\cdot y\,|\,h\in H\}
.

1.2.
Two known results

We begin by proving an elementary result from linear algebra.

Lemma 1.2.1
.

Suppose
F
is a field and
U
is a finite dimensional
F
-vector space. Let
(\vec{u}_{1},\vec{u}_{2},\ldots,\vec{u}_{m})
be a basis for
U
. Suppose
j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
T\,\colon\,U\rightarrow U
is a linear map such that
T(\vec{u}_{k})=(-1)^{j_{k}}\vec{u}_{k+1}
for
1\leq k<m
and
T(\vec{u}_{m})=(-1)^{j_{m}}\vec{u}_{1}
. If
T^{m}=\pm\Id_{U}
, then the characteristic polynomial of
T
is
t^{m}\mp 1
.

Proof.

If
T^{m}=\Id_{U}
, then we conclude that
\sum_{k=1}^{m}j_{k}
is even. If
T^{m}=-\Id_{U}
, then we conclude that
\sum_{k=1}^{m}j_{k}
is odd. Since the characteristic polynomial of
T
is

t^{m}-(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{m}j_{k}},

the result follows.
∎

The next result is well known to experts, but we could not find a reference that did not place conditions on the isogeny class of
G
.

Lemma 1.2.2
.

If
g\in G^{\vartheta-\tor}
, then
g
is
\vartheta
-conjugate in
G
to an element of
A^{\vartheta}
.

Proof.

Recall that an automorphism of
G
is said to be quasi-semisimple if it preserves a Borel-torus pair in
G
.

Since
\vartheta
is quasi-semisimple and
g\rtimes\vartheta
has finite order, it follows that
g\rtimes\vartheta
is quasi-semisimple
[
27
, §7 and §9]
. Hence,
g\rtimes\vartheta
preserves a Borel-torus pair
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
G
.
Since all Borel-torus pairs in
G
are conjugate, there exists
h\in G
such that
h(A,B)=(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
. We then have

h(A,B)=(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})=g\cdot\vartheta(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})=g\vartheta(h)\cdot\vartheta(A,B)=g\vartheta(h)(A,B),

which implies that
h^{-1}g\vartheta(h)\in A
. Thanks to
[
7
, Lemma 7.2]
, we know
A=A^{\vartheta}\cdot(1-\vartheta)A
. The result follows.
∎

2.
An application of the injectivity of
\zeta=\chi_{G}\circ\psi_{G}

A finite group
H
is said to be
rational
provided that for any
h\in H
we have that
h^{j}
is
H
-conjugate to
h
whenever
j
is relatively prime to the order of
h
.
There are many equivalent definitions of rationality, and the nomenclature is explained by the fact
[
25
, Corollary 2 of Section 13.1]
that
H
is rational if and only if every irreducible character of
H
takes values in
\mathbb{Q}
.

It is known that every Weyl group is a rational group (see, for example,
[
15
, Section 3 of Chapter 2 and Chapter 5]
or
[
26
, Theorem 8.5]
). In this section, we give another proof of this fact.

2.1.
Weyl groups are rational

As noted in the Introduction,
the fact that
\zeta
is independent of the isogeny type of
G
together with Theorem
3.1.3
implies

Corollary 2.1.1
.

The map
\zeta\,\colon\,\tilde{W}^{\ee}_{\sim}\rightarrow\kappa_{\sim}
is injective.

Using this, we show:

Lemma 2.1.2
.

Every Weyl group is rational.

Proof.

Suppose
W
is a Weyl group. We will prove this by induction. If
W
is trivial, then there is nothing to prove. Suppose then that
W
is not trivial and the statement is true for every parabolic subgroup of
W
.

Suppose
w\in W
. If the
W
-orbit of
w
intersects a proper parabolic subgroup
W^{\prime}
of
W
, then without loss of generality we may assume that
w\in W^{\prime}
. Since the order of
w
in
W^{\prime}
is equal to the order of
w
in
W
, by induction we have that if
j
is relatively prime to the order of
w
in
W
, then
w^{j}
is
W^{\prime}
-conjugate, hence
W
-conjugate, to
w
.

Suppose now that the
W
-orbit of
w
does not intersect a proper parabolic subgroup of
W
. That is, suppose
w
is elliptic in
W
. It will be enough to show that if
p
is a prime that does not divide the order of
w
, then
w^{p}
and
w
are
W
-conjugate. Fix such a prime
p
.

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a connected reductive group defined over
\mathbb{Z}
with maximal
\mathbb{Z}
-torus
\mathbf{A}
such that
N_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{A})/\mathbf{A}
is
W
. Then
\mathbf{H}
is a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-split group where
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
denotes the
p
-adic completion of
\mathbb{Q}
. Let
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
be an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Let
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\unram}
denote the maximal unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
in
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and let
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\tame}
denote the maximal tame extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
in
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. We have
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\unram}\leq\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\tame}
.
Fix a topological generator
{\Fro}
for
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\unram}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and a topological generator
\sigma
for
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\tame}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\unram})
such that if
{\Fro}
and
\sigma
also denote lifts of these elements into
\Gal({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\tame}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, then
{\Fro}^{-1}\sigma{\Fro}=\sigma^{p}
.

Let
W^{\dtame}
denote the set of tame elements in
W
, that is, those elements
w^{\prime}\in W
such that
p
does not divide the order of
w^{\prime}
. If
w^{\prime}\in W^{\dtame}
, then there exists
h\in\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\tame})
such that the image of
h^{-1}\sigma(h)
in
W
is
w^{\prime}
and the map
w^{\prime}\mapsto{{}^{\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\unram})}\!}({{}^{h}\!}\mathbf{A})
defines a bijective map
\varphi_{\sigma}
from
W^{\dtame}_{\sim}
, the conjugacy classes of tame elements in
W
, to
\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}
, the set of
\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\unram})
-conjugacy classes of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\unram}
-minisotropic maximal
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\unram}
-tori in
\mathbf{H}

[
5
, Lemma 3.4.1]
. A
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\unram}
-torus
\mathbf{T}
in
\mathbf{H}
is said to be
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\unram}
-minisotropic provided that
\X^{*}(\mathbf{T}/Z(\mathbf{H}))^{\Gal(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\unram})}
is trivial.

Since
\mathbf{H}
is
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-split, we have that
{\Fro}
and
\sigma
both act trivially on
W
. Thus, the commutative diagram of bijective maps in
[
5
, Lemma 5.1.2]
becomes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\dtame}_{\sim}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\dtame}_{\sim}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\dtame}_{\sim}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\text{-})^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma^{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Fro}}

Since
p
does not divide the order of
w\in W^{\dtame}
, the diagram implies
{\Fro}(\varphi_{\sigma}({{}^{W}\!}w))=\varphi_{\sigma}({{}^{W}\!}(w^{p}))
.

Fix a
{\Fro}
-stable alcove
D
in the reduced Bruhat-Tits building of
\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\unram})
. From
[
5
, Section 2.2]
we know that
\varphi_{\sigma}({{}^{W}\!}w)
and
{\Fro}(\varphi_{\sigma}({{}^{W}\!}w))
identify unique points, call them
x
and
x^{\prime}
, in the closure of
D
. Note that
x^{\prime}={\Fro}(x)
. From
[
5
, Section 3.5]
the barycentric coordinates of
x
are determined by
\zeta({{}^{W}\!}w)
and those of
x^{\prime}
by
\zeta({{}^{W}\!}(w^{p}))
. Since
\mathbf{H}
is
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-split, we have that
{\Fro}
fixes
D
pointwise. Thus,
x^{\prime}={\Fro}(x)=x
. Thanks to Corollary
2.1.1
, we know that the map
\zeta
is injective. Consequently,
w^{p}
is
W
-conjugate to
w
.
∎

3.
The map
\psi_{G}
is injective

The proof of Theorem
3.1.3
has two steps. In Section
3.1
we show that we may reduce to the case when
G
is isogenous to a simple complex adjoint group. In Sections
3.2
to
3.8
we show that for a given simple complex adjoint group
G^{\prime}
, the map
\psi_{G}
is injective for some
G
that is isogenous to
G^{\prime}
. The case-by-case work we carry out was made much easier thanks to the clearly written material in

[
9
, Sections 3 and 4]
,
[
10
, Section 3.4]
, and
[
11
, Section 2]
.

Remark 3.0.1
.

Note that the order of a lift of a
\vartheta
-elliptic Weyl group element is independent of the lift. Thus, if
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
are
\vartheta
-elliptic elements of
\tilde{W}
that lift to
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
with
n_{1}\rtimes\vartheta
and
n_{2}\rtimes\vartheta
having different orders, then we have
w_{1}
is not
\vartheta
-conjugate to
w_{2}
in
\tilde{W}
. On the other hand, it sometimes happens that
n_{1}\rtimes\vartheta
and
n_{2}\rtimes\vartheta
have the same order, yet
w_{1}
is not
\vartheta
-conjugate to
w_{2}
in
\tilde{W}
. So looking at orders to establish injectivity will not work in general.

3.1.
Reduction to the almost-simple case

We begin by showing that the injectivity of
\psi_{G}
is independent of the isogeny type of
G
.

Lemma 3.1.1
.

Suppose
H
and
I
are semisimple complex groups that are isogenous to
G
. We have that the map

\psi_{H}\,\colon\,\tilde{W}^{\ee}_{\sim}\rightarrow H^{\vartheta-\tor}_{\sim}

is injective if and only if the map

\psi_{I}\,\colon\,\tilde{W}^{\ee}_{\sim}\rightarrow I^{\vartheta-\tor}_{\sim}

is injective.

Proof.

Suppose
\rho\,\colon\,H\rightarrow I
is an isogeny. Let
A_{H}
(resp.
A_{I}
) be a maximal torus in
H
(resp
I
) with
\rho[A_{H}]=A_{I}
.
Fix
w_{1},w_{2}\in\tilde{W}^{\ee}
. Choose
n_{k}\in N_{H}(A_{H})
and
\dot{n}_{k}\in N_{I}(A_{I})
lifting
w_{k}
.

Suppose first that
\psi_{H}
is injective. If there exists
i\in I
such that
i\dot{n}_{1}\vartheta(i)^{-1}=\dot{n}_{2}
, then there exists
h\in H
such that
h{n}_{1}\vartheta(h)^{-1}\in{n}_{2}A_{H}
. Since all lifts of
w_{2}\in\tilde{W}^{\ee}
into
N_{H}(A_{H})
are
\vartheta
-conjugate by an element of
A_{H}
, we may assume that
h{n}_{1}\vartheta(h)^{-1}={n}_{2}
. Since
\psi_{H}
is injective, we conclude that
w_{1}
is
\vartheta
-conjugate to
w_{2}
in
\tilde{W}
. That is,
\psi_{I}
is injective.

Suppose now that that
\psi_{I}
is injective. If there exists
h\in H
such that
h{n}_{1}\vartheta(h)^{-1}={n}_{2}
, then by taking
i=\rho(h)
and
n^{\prime}_{k}=\rho(n_{k})
, we have
i{n}^{\prime}_{1}\vartheta(i)^{-1}={n}^{\prime}_{2}
. Since all lifts of
w_{k}\in\tilde{W}^{\ee}
into
N_{I}(A_{I})
are
\vartheta
-conjugate by an element of
A_{I}
, we may assume that
i\dot{n}_{1}\vartheta(i)^{-1}=\dot{n}_{2}
. Since
\psi_{I}
is injective, we conclude that
w_{1}
is
\vartheta
-conjugate to
w_{2}
in
\tilde{W}
. That is,
\psi_{H}
is injective.
∎

Corollary 3.1.2
.

The map
\psi_{G}
is injective if and only if
\psi_{H}
is injective for every almost-simple factor
H
of
G
.

Proof.

Since the injectivity of
\psi_{G}
is independent of the isogeny type of
G
, we may assume
G
is simply connected. Since
G
is simply connected, we may write
[
17
, Theorem 21.51 and discussion in 24.a]
the
\mathbb{C}
-group
G
as a product of almost-simple simply connected complex groups:
G=G_{1}\times G_{2}\times\cdots\times G_{\tau}
. The result follows.
∎

Theorem 3.1.3
.

The map
\psi_{G}\,\colon\,\tilde{W}^{\ee}_{\sim}\rightarrow G^{\vartheta-\tor}_{\sim}
is injective.

Proof.

Thanks to Corollary
3.1.2
it is enough to show that
\psi_{G^{\prime}}
is injective for
G^{\prime}
being an almost-simple complex group. Thanks to Lemma
3.1.1
, it is enough to show that
\psi_{G}
is injective for some
G
that is isogenous to
G^{\prime}
. This is done on a case-by-case basis in Sections
3.2
to
3.8
.
∎

3.2.
Injectivity of
\psi_{G}
for type
A_{\ell-1}
with
\ell\geq 3

In this situation there is only one elliptic conjugacy class in the Weyl group. Thus, the injectivity of
\psi_{G}
is automatic. However, in the interest of establishing notation in a familiar setting, we carry out a proof that has the same flavor as the proofs for the other root system types.

Let
G=\SL_{\ell}=\SL_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})
realized as the set of
\ell\times\ell
matrices of determinant
1
. The Lie algebra of
G
is then
{\mathfrak{g}}
, the set of
\ell\times\ell
matrices of trace zero. We take
A
to be the diagonal matrices in
G
and
B
to be the upper triangular matrices in
G
. For
a=\Diag(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell})\in A\leq\SL_{\ell}
and
1\leq k<\ell
, we let
\alpha_{k}(a)=a_{k}/a_{k+1}
be the simple roots of
G
with respect to
A
and
B
.

Following Bourbaki
[
2
, Planche I]
we identify
V
with
the hyperplane in
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
for which the sum of the coordinates is zero, identify
\Phi
with the vectors
e_{i}-e_{j}
with
1\leq i\neq j\leq\ell
, and identify
\Delta
with the roots
\alpha_{k}=e_{k}-e_{k+1}
for
1\leq k<\ell
.

For
1\leq k<\ell
we define
s_{k}\in\SL_{\ell}
to be the
\ell\times\ell
matrix such that

(s_{k})_{ij}=\begin{cases}1&\qquad\text{$i=j$ and $i\not\in\{k,k+1\}$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i=k$ and $j=k+1$}\\
-1&\qquad\text{$i=k+1$ and $j=k$}\\
0&\qquad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The image of the order four element
s_{k}
in
\tilde{W}
is the simple reflection corresponding to
\alpha_{k}
.

The Coxeter class is the only elliptic conjugacy class in
\tilde{W}
, and it corresponds to the partition
\vec{\ell}=(\ell)
of
\ell
. Define
n_{({\ell})}=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}s_{j}
. This is a lift of a Coxeter element in
\tilde{W}
, and it has order
2\ell
if
\ell
is even or
\ell
if
\ell
is odd.

Any element
g\in\SL_{\ell}
acts on
\mathbb{C}^{\ell}
by matrix multiplication. The characteristic polynomial of
g
with respect to this action is an
\SL_{\ell}
-conjugation invariant polynomial. Thanks to Lemma
1.2.1
the characteristic polynomial of
n_{({\ell})}
with respect to this action is

q_{n_{({\ell})}}(t)=t^{\ell}+(-1)^{\ell}
.

Since any two lifts of an elliptic element in
\tilde{W}
are
A
-conjugate and since there exists a unique conjugacy class of elliptic elements in
\tilde{W}
, we conclude that two elliptic elements of
\tilde{W}
are
\tilde{W}
-conjugate if and only if their lifts are
\SL_{\ell}
-conjugate.

3.3.
Injectivity of
\psi_{G}
for type
B_{\ell}
with
\ell\geq 2

Let
G={\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}={\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}(\mathbb{C})
realized as those
g\in\SL_{2\ell+1}
such that
g=(Kg^{-1}K)^{T}
. Recall that
K
denotes the
(2\ell+1)\times(2\ell+1)
matrix with ones on the anti-diagonal and zeroes elsewhere.
Then
{\mathfrak{g}}
, the Lie algebra of
G
, is
\mathfrak{so}_{2\ell+1}
realized as the Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2\ell+1}
consisting of those matrices
X
for which
X=-KX^{T}K
. We take
A
to be the diagonal matrices in
G
and
B
to be the upper triangular matrices in
G
. For
a=\Diag(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell},1,a_{\ell}^{-1},\ldots,a_{2}^{-1},a_{1}^{-1})\in A\leq{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}
, we let
\alpha_{\ell}(a)=a_{\ell}
and
\alpha_{k}(a)=a_{k}/a_{k+1}
for
1\leq k<\ell
be the simple roots of
G
with respect to
A
and
B
.

Following Bourbaki
[
2
, Planche II]
, we identify
V
with
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, identify
\Phi
with the set of vectors
\pm e_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
and
\pm e_{i}\pm e_{j}
for
1\leq i<j\leq\ell
, and identify
\Delta
with

\alpha_{1}=e_{1}-e_{2},\,\alpha_{2}=e_{2}-e_{3},\,\ldots,\,\alpha_{\ell-1}=e_{\ell-1}-e_{\ell},\,\alpha_{\ell}=e_{\ell}.

We define
t_{\ell}\in{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}
to be the
(2\ell+1)\times(2\ell+1)
matrix such that

(t_{\ell})_{ij}=\begin{cases}1&\qquad\text{$1\leq i=j<\ell$ or $\ell+2<i=j\leq 2\ell+1$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i\neq j$ and $i,j\in\{\ell,\ell+2\}$}\\
-1&\qquad\text{$i=j=\ell+1$}\\
0&\qquad\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

and for
1\leq k<\ell
we define
s_{k}\in{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}
to be the
(2\ell+1)\times(2\ell+1)
matrix such that

(s_{k})_{ij}=\begin{cases}1&\qquad\text{$i=j$ and $i\not\in\{k,k+1,2\ell-k+2,2\ell-k+1\}$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i\neq j$ and $i,j\in\{k,k+1\}$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i\neq j$ and $i,j\in\{2\ell-k+1,2\ell-k+2\}$}\\
0&\qquad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The image of the order two element
t_{\ell}
in
\tilde{W}
is the simple reflection corresponding to
\alpha_{\ell}
while the image of the order two element
s_{k}
in
\tilde{W}
is the simple reflection corresponding to
\alpha_{k}
. For
1\leq k<\ell
define
t_{k}\in{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}
by

t_{k}=s_{k}\cdot s_{k+1}\cdot s_{k+2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{\ell-2}\cdot s_{\ell-1}\cdot t_{\ell}\cdot s_{\ell-1}\cdot s_{\ell-2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{k+1}\cdot s_{k}.

Note that the
t_{k}
defined above is denoted by
t_{k-1}
in
[
10
, §3.4]
.

The elliptic conjugacy classes are parameterized by the partitions of
\ell

[
12
, 7.16]
.
Fix a partition
\vec{\ell}=(\ell_{\mu}\geq\ell_{\mu-1}\geq\cdots\geq\ell_{1})
of
\ell
. Recall that for
1\leq\nu\leq\mu
we define
\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}
=
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\cdots+\ell_{\nu-1}
. We define
n_{\vec{\ell}}
to be the product
c_{1}\cdot c_{2}\cdot\cdots\cdot c_{\mu}
where

c_{\nu}=s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+1}\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}-1}\cdot t_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}}
.
Thus

n_{\vec{\ell}}=\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+1}\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}-1}\cdot t_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}}).

The image of
n_{\vec{\ell}}
in
\tilde{W}
is an elliptic element belonging to the conjugacy class associated to
\vec{\ell}
.

Remark 3.3.1
.

Since
w\in\tilde{W}
is regular elliptic if and only if
{\mathfrak{h}}^{w}=\{0\}
and
\langle w\rangle
acts freely on
\Phi
, the regular elliptic elements of
\tilde{W}
correspond to partitions of the form
(d,d,\ldots,d)
where
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
divides
\ell
.

Any element
g\in{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}
acts on
\mathbb{C}^{2\ell+1}
by matrix multiplication. The characteristic polynomial of
g
with respect to this action is an
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}
-conjugation invariant polynomial. Using Lemma
1.2.1
, one finds that the characteristic polynomial of
n_{\vec{\ell}}
with respect to this action is

q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}(t)=(t-(-1)^{\mu})\cdot\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t^{2\ell_{\nu}}-1).

If
\vec{\ell}
and
\vec{\ell}^{\prime}
are two partitions of
\ell
for which the associated elements
n_{\vec{\ell}}

and
n_{\vec{\ell}^{\prime}}
of
N_{G}(A)
are
G
-conjugate, then we have
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}=q_{n_{\vec{\ell}^{\prime}}}
. The only way this can happen is if
\vec{\ell}=\vec{\ell}^{\prime}
.

Since any two lifts of an elliptic element in
\tilde{W}
are
A
-conjugate, we conclude that two elliptic elements of
\tilde{W}
are
\tilde{W}
-conjugate if and only if their lifts are
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}
-conjugate.

3.4.
Injectivity of
\psi_{G}
for type
C_{\ell}
with
\ell\geq 2

Recall that
K
denotes the
\ell\times\ell
matrix with ones on the anti-diagonal and zeroes elsewhere. Denote by
J
the product of
K
and
\Diag(1,1,\ldots,1,1,-1,-1,\ldots,-1,-1)
; here there are
\ell
copies of
1
followed by
\ell
copies of
-1
. We realize
G={\mathrm{Sp}}_{2\ell}={\mathrm{Sp}}_{2\ell}(\mathbb{C})
as those
g\in\SL_{2\ell}
such that
g^{T}Jg=J
and we realize
\mathfrak{sp}_{2\ell}
as those
X\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2\ell}
such that
X^{T}J=-JX
.
We take
A
to be the diagonal matrices in
G
and
B
to be the upper triangular matrices in
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2\ell}
. For
a=\Diag(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell},a_{\ell}^{-1},\ldots,a_{2}^{-1},a_{1}^{-1})\in A
, we let
\alpha_{\ell}(a)=a_{\ell}^{2}
and
\alpha_{k}(a)=a_{k}/a_{k+1}
for
1\leq k<\ell
be the simple roots of
G
with respect to
A
and
B
.

Following Bourbaki
[
2
, Planche III]
, we identify
V
with
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, identify
\Phi
with
the set of vectors
\pm 2e_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
and
\pm e_{i}\pm e_{j}
for
1\leq i<j\leq\ell
, and identify
\Delta
with the roots

\alpha_{1}=e_{1}-e_{2},\,\alpha_{2}=e_{2}-e_{3},\,\ldots,\,\alpha_{\ell-1}=e_{\ell-1}-e_{\ell},\,\alpha_{\ell}=2e_{\ell}.

We define
t_{\ell}\in{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2\ell}
to be the
2\ell\times 2\ell
matrix such that

(t_{\ell})_{ij}=\begin{cases}1&\qquad\text{$1\leq i=j<\ell$ or $\ell+1<i=j\leq 2\ell$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i=\ell$ and $j=\ell+1$}\\
-1&\qquad\text{$i=\ell+1$ and $j=\ell$}\\
0&\qquad\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

and for
1\leq k<\ell
we define
s_{k}\in{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2\ell}
to be the
2\ell\times 2\ell
matrix such that

(s_{k})_{ij}=\begin{cases}1&\qquad\text{$i=j$ and $i\not\in\{k,k+1,2\ell-k+1,2\ell-k\}$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i\neq j$ and $i,j\in\{k,k+1\}$ }\\
1&\qquad\text{$i\neq j$ and $i,j\in\{2\ell-k,2\ell-k+1\}$ }\\
0&\qquad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The image of the order four element
t_{\ell}
in
\tilde{W}
is the simple reflection corresponding to
\alpha_{\ell}
while the image of the order two element
s_{k}
in
\tilde{W}
is the simple reflection corresponding to
\alpha_{k}
. For
1\leq k<\ell
define
t_{k}\in{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2\ell}
by

t_{k}=s_{k}\cdot s_{k+1}\cdot s_{k+2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{\ell-2}\cdot s_{\ell-1}\cdot t_{\ell}\cdot s_{\ell-1}\cdot s_{\ell-2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{k+1}\cdot s_{k}.

Note that the
t_{k}
defined above is denoted by
t_{k-1}
in
[
10
, §3.4]
.

Since the Weyl group of type
C_{\ell}
may be naturally identified with the Weyl group of type
B_{\ell}
,
the elliptic conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}
correspond to partitions
\vec{\ell}=(\ell_{\mu}\geq\ell_{\mu-1}\geq\cdots\geq\ell_{1})
of
\ell

[
12
, 7.16]
.
Recall that for
1\leq\nu\leq\mu
we define
\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}
=
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\cdots+\ell_{\nu-1}
. We define
n_{\vec{\ell}}
to be the product
c_{1}\cdot c_{2}\cdot\cdots\cdot c_{\mu}
where

c_{\nu}=s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+1}\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}-1}\cdot t_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}}
.
Thus

n_{\vec{\ell}}=\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+1}\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}-1}\cdot t_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}}).

The image of
n_{\vec{\ell}}
in
\tilde{W}
is an elliptic element belonging to the conjugacy class associated to
\vec{\ell}
.

Remark 3.4.1
.

Since
w\in\tilde{W}
is regular elliptic if and only if
{\mathfrak{h}}^{w}=\{0\}
and
\langle w\rangle
acts freely on
\Phi
, the regular elliptic elements of
\tilde{W}
correspond to partitions of the form
(d,d,\ldots,d)
where
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
divides
\ell
.

Any element
g\in{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2\ell}
acts on
\mathbb{C}^{2\ell}
by matrix multiplication. The characteristic polynomial of
g
with respect to this action is an
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2\ell}
-conjugation invariant polynomial. Using Lemma
1.2.1
, one finds that the characteristic polynomial of
n_{\vec{\ell}}
with respect to this action is

q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}(t)=\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t^{2\ell_{\nu}}+1).

If
\vec{\ell}
and
\vec{\ell}^{\prime}
are two partitions of
\ell
for which the associated elements
n_{\vec{\ell}}

and
n_{\vec{\ell}^{\prime}}
of
N_{G}(A)
are
G
-conjugate, then we have
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}=q_{n_{\vec{\ell}^{\prime}}}
. The only way this can happen is if
\vec{\ell}=\vec{\ell}^{\prime}
.

Since any two lifts of an elliptic element in
\tilde{W}
are
A
-conjugate, we conclude that two elliptic elements of
\tilde{W}
are
\tilde{W}
-conjugate if and only if their lifts are
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2\ell}
-conjugate.

3.5.
Injectivity of
\psi_{G}
for type
D_{\ell}
with
\ell\geq 3

Let
G={\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell}={\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell}(\mathbb{C})
realized as those
g\in\GL_{2\ell}
such that
g=(Kg^{-1}K)^{T}
and
\det(g)=1
. If we remove the determinant one condition, then
g
is an element of
\On_{2\ell}=\On_{2\ell}(\mathbb{C})
. Then
{\mathfrak{g}}
, the Lie algebra of
G
, is
\mathfrak{so}_{2\ell}
realized as the Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2\ell}
consisting of those matrices
X
for which
X=-KX^{T}K
. We take
A
to be the diagonal matrices in
G
and
B
to be the upper triangular matrices in
G
. For
a=\Diag(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell},a_{\ell}^{-1},\ldots,a_{2}^{-1},a_{1}^{-1})\in A
, we let
\alpha_{\ell}(a)=a_{\ell-1}\cdot a_{\ell}
and
\alpha_{k}(a)=a_{k}/a_{k+1}
for
1\leq k<\ell
be the simple roots of
G
with respect to
A
and
B
.

Following Bourbaki
[
2
, Planche IV]
we identify
V
with
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, identify
\Phi
with the vectors
\pm e_{i}\pm e_{j}
for
1\leq i<j\leq\ell
, and identify
\Delta
with the roots

\alpha_{1}=e_{1}-e_{2},\,\alpha_{2}=e_{2}-e_{3},\,\ldots,\,\alpha_{\ell-1}=e_{\ell-1}-e_{\ell},\,\alpha_{\ell}=e_{\ell-1}+e_{\ell}.

For
1\leq k\leq\ell
we define
t_{k}\in\On_{2\ell}
to be the
2\ell\times 2\ell
matrix such that

(t_{k})_{ij}=\begin{cases}1&\qquad\text{$1\leq i=j\leq 2l$ and $i,j\not\in\{k,2\ell-k+1\}$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i\neq j$ and $i,j\in\{k,2\ell-k+1\}$}\\
0&\qquad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

For
1\leq k<\ell
we define
s_{k}\in{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell}
to be the
2\ell\times 2\ell
matrix such that

(s_{k})_{ij}=\begin{cases}1&\qquad\text{$i=j$ and $i\not\in\{k,k+1,2\ell-k+1,2\ell-k\}$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i\neq j$ and $i,j\in\{k,k+1\}$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i\neq j$ and $i,j\in\{2\ell-k,2\ell-k+1\}$}\\
0&\qquad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The image of the order two element
s_{k}
in
\tilde{W}
is the simple reflection corresponding to
\alpha_{k}
.

The elliptic conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}
correspond to partitions
\vec{\ell}=(\ell_{\mu}\geq\ell_{\mu-1}\geq\cdots\geq\ell_{1})
of
\ell
where
\mu
is even
[
12
, 7.20]
. Fix such a partition. Recall that for
1\leq\nu\leq\mu
we define
\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}
=
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\cdots+\ell_{\nu-1}
. We define
{n}_{\vec{\ell}}
to be the product
c_{1}\cdot c_{2}\cdot\cdots\cdot c_{\mu}
where

c_{\nu}=s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+1}\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}-1}\cdot t_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}}
.
Note that
c_{\nu}\in\On_{2\ell}
, but since
\mu
is even we have that
n_{\vec{\ell}}
belongs to
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell}
.
We have

{n}_{\vec{\ell}}=\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+1}\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}-1}\cdot t_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}}).

Let
\mathcal{O}_{\vec{\ell}}
denote the conjugacy class in
\tilde{W}
of the image of
{n}_{\vec{\ell}}
in
\tilde{W}
. The correspondence
\vec{\ell}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\vec{\ell}}
defines a bijective correspondence between partitions of
\ell
into an even number of parts and
\vartheta
-elliptic
\vartheta
-conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}
.

Remark 3.5.1
.

Since
w\in\tilde{W}
is regular elliptic if and only if
{\mathfrak{h}}^{w}=\{0\}
and
\langle w\rangle
acts freely on
\Phi
, the regular elliptic elements of
\tilde{W}
correspond to partitions of the form
(d,d,\ldots,d)
where
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
divides
\ell
and
\frac{\ell}{d}
is even or
(e,e,\ldots,e,e,1)
where
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
divides
\ell-1
and
\frac{\ell-1}{e}
is odd.

Any element
g\in{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell}
acts on
\mathbb{C}^{2\ell}
by matrix multiplication. The characteristic polynomial of
g
with respect to this action is an
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell}
-conjugation invariant polynomial. Using Lemma
1.2.1
, one finds that the characteristic polynomial of
n_{\vec{\ell}}
with respect to this action is

q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}(t)=\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t^{2\ell_{\nu}}-1).

If
\vec{\ell}
and
\vec{\ell}^{\prime}
are two partitions of
\ell
for which the associated elements
n_{\vec{\ell}}

and
n_{\vec{\ell}^{\prime}}
of
N_{G}(A)
are
G
-conjugate, then we have
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}=q_{n_{\vec{\ell}^{\prime}}}
. The only way this can happen is if
\vec{\ell}=\vec{\ell}^{\prime}
.

Since any two lifts of an elliptic element in
\tilde{W}
are
A
-conjugate, we conclude that two elliptic elements of
\tilde{W}
are
\tilde{W}
-conjugate if and only if their lifts are
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell}
-conjugate.

3.6.
Injectivity of
\psi_{G}
for type
{{}^{2}\!}A_{\ell-1}
with
\ell\geq 3

We adopt the notation of Section
3.2
. In particular, we have
G=\SL_{\ell}
,
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sl}_{\ell}
, and the roots in
\Delta
are
\alpha_{k}:=e_{k}-e_{k+1}
for
1\leq k<\ell
.

We begin by defining an involution of
G
, and hence
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Let
K
denote the
\ell\times\ell
matrix with ones on the anti-diagonal and zeroes elsewhere.
We define an order two element
J
as follows. If
\ell
is odd, then
J=K
. If
\ell
is even, then
J
is the product of
K
with
\Diag(i,i,\ldots,i,i,-i,-i,\ldots,-i,-i)
; here there are
\ell/2
copies of
i
followed by
\ell/2
copies of
-i
.
In all cases we have that
\det(J)=(-1)^{\lfloor\frac{\ell}{2}\rfloor}
; that is,
J
has determinant
1
if
\ell
is congruent to
0
or
1
modulo
4
and is
-1
otherwise. We also have that
J
, and hence
\Ad(J)
, always has order two.
We define the involution
\vartheta
on
G
by
\vartheta(g)=\Ad(J)(g^{-1})^{T}
for
g\in G
. We abuse notation and denote the resulting involution on
{\mathfrak{g}}
by
\vartheta
as well. Note that
\vartheta(X)=-\Ad(J)(X^{T})=-J{X}^{T}J
for
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}
.

For
1\leq k<\ell
we have
s_{k}\in\SL_{\ell}
as in Section
3.2
and we define
\tilde{s}_{k}\in\GL_{\ell}
to be the
\ell\times\ell
matrix

(\tilde{s}_{k})_{ij}=\begin{cases}1&\qquad\text{$i=j$ and $i\not\in\{k,k+1\}$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i=k$ and $j=k+1$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i=k+1$ and $j=k$}\\
0&\qquad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The image of the order four element
s_{k}
in
\tilde{W}
is the simple reflection corresponding to
\alpha_{k}
.

Suppose

\vec{\ell}=\ell_{\mu}\geq\ell_{\mu-1}\geq\cdots\geq\ell_{1}
is a partition of
\ell
for which each
\ell_{\nu}
is odd. Recall that
\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}=\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\cdots+\ell_{\nu-1}
. For
1<\nu\leq\mu
, set

c_{\nu}=s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+1}\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+2}\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+3}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+(\ell_{\nu}-2)}\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+(\ell_{\nu}-1)}.

For
1<\nu\leq\mu
, the element
c_{\nu}
has order
\ell_{\nu}
.
If
\ell
modulo
4
is
0
or
1
, then define

c_{1}=s_{1}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{3}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{(\ell_{1}-2)}\cdot s_{(\ell_{1}-1)}

and note that this element has order
\ell_{1}
.
If
\ell
modulo
4
is
2
or
3
and
\ell_{1}>1
, then define

c_{1}=s_{1}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{3}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{(\ell_{1}-2)}\cdot\tilde{s}_{(\ell_{1}-1)}.

If
\ell
modulo
4
is
2
or
3
and
\ell_{1}=1
, then define
c_{1}=\Diag(-1,1,1,1,\ldots,1,1,1)
.
Note that if
\ell
is congruent to
2
or
3
modulo
4
, then the order of
c_{1}
is
2\ell_{1}
.

Define
\tilde{n}_{\vec{\ell}}=c_{1}\cdot c_{2}\cdot\cdots\cdot c_{\mu}
. We have
n_{\vec{\ell}}:=\tilde{n}_{\vec{\ell}}J\in\SL_{\ell}
. Let
w_{\vec{\ell}}
denote the image of
n_{\vec{\ell}}
in
\tilde{W}
and let
\mathcal{O}_{\vec{\ell}}
denote the
\vartheta
-conjugacy class of
w_{\vec{\ell}}
in
\tilde{W}
. The correspondence
\vec{\ell}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\vec{\ell}}
between partitions of
\ell
into odd parts and
\vartheta
-elliptic
\vartheta
-conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}
is a bijection
[
12
, 7.14]
.
Moreover,
n_{\vec{\ell}}
is a lift of
w_{\vec{\ell}}
into
N_{G}(A)
.

Remark 3.6.1
.

Since
w\in\tilde{W}
is regular
\vartheta
-elliptic if and only if
{\mathfrak{h}}^{w\rtimes\vartheta}=\{0\}
and
\langle w\rtimes\vartheta\rangle
acts freely on
\Phi
, the regular
\vartheta
-elliptic elements of
\tilde{W}
correspond to partitions of the form
(d,d,\ldots,d,d,1)
where
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
is odd and divides
\ell-1
or
(e,e,\ldots,e)
where
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
is odd and divides
\ell

We wish to calculate the characteristic polynomial,
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}
, of the linear map
T_{\vec{\ell}}\in\Hom(\mathfrak{sl}_{\ell},\mathfrak{sl}_{\ell})
defined by
T_{\vec{\ell}}(X)=\Ad(n_{\vec{\ell}})\vartheta(X)=-\Ad(n_{\vec{\ell}}J)(X^{T})=-\Ad(\tilde{n}_{\vec{\ell}})(X^{T})
.
Since
\vartheta\circ\Ad(g)=\Ad(\vartheta(g))\circ\vartheta
for all
g\in\GL_{\ell}
, it follows that
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}=q_{gn_{\vec{\ell}}\vartheta(g^{-1})}
for all
g\in\SL_{\ell}
.

Lemma 3.6.2
.

We have

q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}(t)=\dfrac{1}{t+1}\cdot\left(\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t^{\ell_{\nu}}+1)\right)\cdot\left(\prod_{\tau=1}^{\mu}(t^{2\ell_{\tau}}-1)^{(\ell_{\tau}-1)/2}\right)\cdot\left(\prod_{1\leq\rho<\sigma\leq\mu}(t^{2\lcm(\ell_{\rho},\ell_{\sigma})}-1)^{\gcd(\ell_{\rho},\ell_{\sigma})}\right).

Proof.

By identifying the standard ordered basis of
\mathbb{C}^{\ell_{\nu}}
with
(e_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+1},e_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+2},\ldots,e_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}-1},e_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}})
we have an embedding of
\GL_{\ell_{\nu}}
into
\GL_{\ell}
.
Under this identification, we think of
c_{\nu}
as being an element of
\GL_{\ell_{\nu}}
, and hence
\tilde{n}_{\vec{\ell}}\in\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}\GL_{\ell_{\nu}}
. Note that the image of
c_{\nu}
in the Weyl group of
\GL_{\ell_{\nu}}
is a Coxeter element, hence has order
\ell_{\nu}
in this Weyl group.

For
1\leq\rho,\sigma\leq\mu
we define the block
B_{\rho,\sigma}
to be the vector subspace of
\mathfrak{sl}_{\ell}
consisting of matrices
X
for which
X_{ij}\neq 0
if and only if
i\in\{\ell^{\prime}_{\rho}+1,\ell^{\prime}_{\rho}+2,\ldots,\ell^{\prime}_{\rho}+\ell_{\rho}-1\}

and
j\in\{\ell^{\prime}_{\sigma}+1,\ell^{\prime}_{\sigma}+2,\ldots,\ell^{\prime}_{\sigma}+\ell_{\sigma}-1\}
.
We let
S_{0}
denote the sum of diagonal blocks, that is
S_{0}=\sum_{1\leq\nu\leq\mu}B_{\nu\nu}
. We identify
S_{0}
with the Lie algebra of the group of determinant one matrices in
\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}\GL_{\ell_{\nu}}
.
For
1\leq\rho<\sigma\leq\mu
, we define the vector subspace
S_{\rho\sigma}
of
\mathfrak{sl}_{\ell}
by
S_{\rho\sigma}=B_{\rho\sigma}+B_{\sigma\rho}
. If we think of
\GL_{\ell_{\rho}}\times GL_{\ell_{\sigma}}
as a subgroup of
\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}\GL_{\ell_{\nu}}\subset\GL_{\ell}
in the natural way, then
S_{\rho\sigma}
is a
(\GL_{\ell_{\rho}}\times GL_{\ell_{\sigma}})\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
-module where
\vartheta^{\prime}(X)=-X^{T}
.

Treating
\mathfrak{sl}_{\ell}
as a
(\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}\GL_{\ell_{\nu}})\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
-module, we have

\mathfrak{sl}_{\ell}=S_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{1\leq\rho<\sigma\leq\mu}S_{\rho\sigma}.

The polynomial
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}
is the product of the characteristic polynomials for the action of
\tilde{n}_{\vec{\ell}}\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
on each of the
(\mu^{2}-\mu)/2+1
summands above. We now calculate these characteristic polynomials.

The
(\GL_{\ell_{\rho}}\times\GL_{\ell_{\sigma}})\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
-module
S_{\rho\sigma}
is also an
A
-module and, as such, may be written as a direct sum of root spaces. The image of
c_{\rho}c_{\sigma}\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
in the twisted Weyl group of
(\GL_{\ell_{\rho}}\times\GL_{\ell_{\sigma}})\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
acts freely on these roots, with orbits of size
2\lcm(\ell_{\rho},\ell_{\sigma})
. Since the dimension of
S_{\rho\sigma}
is
2\ell_{\rho}\ell_{\sigma}
, there are
\gcd(\ell_{\rho},\ell_{\sigma})
of these orbits. Since the image of
(c_{\rho}c_{\sigma})^{2\lcm(\ell_{\rho},\ell_{\sigma})}
in
A
is trivial, it follows from Lemma
1.2.1
that
the characteristic polynomial of
\Ad(c_{\rho}c_{\sigma})\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
, hence of
\Ad(\tilde{n}_{\vec{\ell}})\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
, acting on
S_{\rho,\sigma}
is
(t^{2\lcm(\ell_{\rho},\ell_{\sigma})}-1)^{\gcd(\ell_{\rho},\ell_{\sigma})}
.

We now calculate the characteristic polynomial for the action of
\tilde{n}_{\vec{\ell}}\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
acting on
S_{0}
. Since
\tilde{n}_{\vec{\ell}}\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
fixes the center of
\mathfrak{gl}_{\ell_{\nu}}
, this characteristic polynomial
is equal to
\frac{1}{t+1}
times the product for
1\leq\nu\leq\mu
of the characteristic polynomials for the action of
c_{\nu}\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
on
\mathfrak{gl}_{\ell_{\nu}}
. Since the transpose operation is trivial on
\mathfrak{d}_{\nu}
, the set of diagonal elements in
\mathfrak{gl}_{\ell_{\nu}}
, the action of
c_{\nu}\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
factors through to an action of
w_{\nu}\rtimes-1
where
w_{\nu}
is a Coxeter element in
\tilde{W}_{\nu}
, the Weyl group of
\GL_{\ell_{\nu}}
. Thus the characteristic polynomial for the action
c_{\nu}\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
on
\mathfrak{d}_{\nu}
is
(t^{\ell_{\nu}}+1)
. The image of
c_{\nu}\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
in
\tilde{W}_{\nu}\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
acts freely on the roots of
\GL_{\ell_{\nu}}
with respect to
A
. Since the orbits for this action have
2\ell_{\nu}
elements and the dimension of
\mathfrak{gl}_{2\ell_{\nu}}/\mathfrak{d}_{\nu}
is
\ell_{\nu}^{2}-\ell_{\nu}
, there are
(\ell_{\nu}-1)/2
of these orbits. Since
c_{\nu}^{2\ell_{\nu}}
is trivial, it follows from Lemma
1.2.1
that the characteristic polynomial for the action
c_{\nu}\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
on
\mathfrak{gl}_{\ell_{\nu}}(\mathbb{C})/\mathfrak{d}_{\nu}

is
(t^{2\ell_{\nu}}-1)^{(\ell_{\nu}-1)/2}
. Thus, the characteristic polynomial for the action of
\tilde{n}_{\vec{\ell}}\rtimes\vartheta^{\prime}
acting on
S_{0}
is

\frac{1}{t+1}\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t^{\ell_{\nu}}+1)\prod_{\tau=1}^{\mu}(t^{2\ell_{\tau}}-1)^{(\ell_{\tau}-1)/2}
.
∎

Lemma 3.6.3
.

If
\vec{\ell}
and
\vec{\ell}^{\prime}
are two partitions of
\ell
with
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}=q_{n_{\vec{\ell}^{\prime}}}
, then
\vec{\ell}=\vec{\ell}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\vec{\ell}
is a partition of
\ell
into odd parts.
List out all the roots of
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}
and call the list
S
. The list
S
has
\dim({\mathfrak{g}})
entries. There exists an
m\in\mathbb{N}
so that every element of
S
looks like
\xi^{\nu}_{2m}
where
\xi_{2m}
is a fixed primitive
(2m)^{\text{th}}
root of unity and
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
. Choose the largest odd
h\in\mathbb{N}
for which there exists a primitive
(2h)^{\text{th}}
root of unity, call it
\xi
, in
S
with the property that every power of
\xi
occurs in
S
. For such a
\xi
remove one copy of each power of
\xi
from
S
– note, we are only removing
2h
elements from
S
. Continue this process until it cannot be repeated – that is, until the list
S
consists of roots of unity
\xi
such that
\xi^{j}
is not in
S
for some
j
. The number of times this process can happen is equal to the number of times one appears as a root of
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}
.

There will be
\ell-1
roots remaining, and these will be the roots of

(3.6.4)

p_{\vec{\ell}}(t):=\frac{1}{t+1}\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t^{\ell_{\nu}}+1).

That is, this algorithm recovers
p_{\vec{\ell}}
from
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}
, and hence it recovers
\vec{\ell}
.
∎

By construction, we have
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}=q_{xn_{\vec{\ell}}\vartheta(x^{-1})}
for all
x\in G
. Since any two lifts of a
\vartheta
-elliptic element in
\tilde{W}
to elements in
N_{G}(A)\rtimes\vartheta
are
\vartheta
-conjugate by an element of
A
, we conclude from Lemma
3.6.3
that two
\vartheta
-elliptic elements of
\tilde{W}
are
\vartheta
-conjugate in
\tilde{W}
if and only if their lifts into
N_{G}(A)
are
\vartheta
-conjugate in
\SL_{\ell}
.

3.7.
Injectivity of
\psi_{G}
for type
{{}^{2}\!}D_{\ell+1}
with
\ell\geq 2

Let
G={\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+2}
and let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be
\mathfrak{so}_{2\ell+2}
realized as in Section
3.5
. We take
A
to be the diagonal matrices in
G
and
B
to be the upper triangular matrices in
G
. We realize
V
,
\Phi
, and
\Delta
as in Section
3.5
. In particular, the elements of
\Delta
are

\alpha_{1}=e_{1}-e_{2},\,\alpha_{2}=e_{2}-e_{3},\,\ldots,\,\alpha_{\ell-1}=e_{\ell-1}-e_{\ell},\alpha_{\ell}=e_{\ell}-e_{\ell+1},\,\alpha_{\ell+1}=e_{\ell}+e_{\ell+1}.

Let
J
be the
(2\ell+2)\times(2\ell+2)
matrix such that
J_{ii}=1
for
i\neq\ell+1,\ell+2
,
J_{\ell+1,\ell+2}=J_{\ell+2,\ell+1}=1
, and all other entries are zero. Then
J\in\On_{2\ell+2}
. We define an involution
\vartheta
on
{\mathfrak{g}}
by
\vartheta(X)=JXJ
.

For
1\leq k\leq\ell
we define
s_{k}\in{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+2}
to be the
(2\ell+2)\times(2\ell+2)
matrix such that

(s_{k})_{ij}=\begin{cases}1&\qquad\text{$i=j$ and $i\not\in\{k,k+1,2\ell-k+3,2\ell-k+2\}$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i\neq j$ and $i,j\in\{k,k+1\}$}\\
1&\qquad\text{$i\neq j$ and $i,j\in\{2\ell-k+2,2\ell-k+3\}$}\\
0&\qquad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The image of the order two element
s_{k}
in
\tilde{W}
is the simple reflection corresponding to
\alpha_{k}
. For
1\leq k\leq\ell+1
define
t_{k}\in\On_{2\ell+2}
by

t_{k}=s_{k}\cdot s_{k+1}\cdot s_{k+2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{\ell-2}\cdot s_{\ell-1}\cdot s_{\ell}\cdot J\cdot s_{\ell}\cdot s_{\ell-1}\cdot s_{\ell-2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{k+1}\cdot s_{k}.

Note that
t_{\ell+1}=J
.

The
\vartheta
-elliptic conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}
correspond to partitions
\vec{\ell}=(\ell_{\mu}\geq\ell_{\mu-1}\geq\cdots\geq\ell_{1})
of
\ell+1
where
\mu
is odd
[
12
, 7.20]
. Fix such a partition. Recall that for
1\leq\nu\leq\mu
we define
\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}
=
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\cdots+\ell_{\nu-1}
. We define
\tilde{n}_{\vec{\ell}}
to be the product
c_{1}\cdot c_{2}\cdot\cdots\cdot c_{\mu}
where

c_{\nu}=s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+1}\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}-1}\cdot t_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}}
.
Thus

\tilde{n}_{\vec{\ell}}=\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+1}\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+2}\cdot\cdots\cdot s_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}-1}\cdot t_{\ell^{\prime}_{\nu}+\ell_{\nu}}).

Let
\mathcal{O}_{\vec{\ell}}
denote the
\vartheta
-conjugacy class in
\tilde{W}
of the image of
{n}_{\vec{\ell}}=\tilde{n}_{\vec{\ell}}J
in
\tilde{W}
. The correspondence
\vec{\ell}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\vec{\ell}}
defines a bijective correspondence between partitions of
\ell+1
with an odd number of parts and
\vartheta
-elliptic
\vartheta
-conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}
.

Remark 3.7.1
.

Since
w\in\tilde{W}
is regular
\vartheta
-elliptic if and only if
{\mathfrak{h}}^{w\rtimes\vartheta}=\{0\}
and
\langle w\rtimes\vartheta\rangle
acts freely on
\Phi
, the regular
\vartheta
-elliptic elements of
\tilde{W}
correspond to partitions of the form
(d,d,\ldots,d)
where
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
divides
\ell+1
and
\frac{\ell+1}{d}
is odd or
(e,e,\ldots,e,e,1)
where
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
divides
\ell
and
\frac{\ell}{e}
is even.

Any element
g\in{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+2}
acts on
\mathbb{C}^{2\ell+2}
by matrix multiplication. The characteristic polynomial, call it
q_{g}
, of
gJ
with respect to this action is an
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+2}
-conjugation invariant polynomial, hence
q_{g}=q_{xg\vartheta(x^{-1})}
for all
g,x\in{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+2}
.
Using Lemma
1.2.1
, one finds that

q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}(t)=\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t^{2\ell_{\nu}}-1).

If
\vec{\ell}
and
\vec{\ell}^{\prime}
are two partitions of
\ell
for which the associated elements
n_{\vec{\ell}}

and
n_{\vec{\ell}^{\prime}}
of
N_{G}(A)
are
\vartheta
-conjugate in
G
, then we have
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}=q_{n_{\vec{\ell}^{\prime}}}
. The only way this can happen is if
\vec{\ell}=\vec{\ell}^{\prime}
.

Since any two lifts of an elliptic element in
\tilde{W}\rtimes\vartheta
into
N_{G}(A)\rtimes\vartheta
are
A
-conjugate, we conclude that two
\vartheta
-elliptic elements of
\tilde{W}
are
\vartheta
-conjugate in
\tilde{W}
if and only if their lifts into
N_{G}(A)
are
\vartheta
-conjugate in
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+2}
.

3.8.
Injectivity of
\psi_{G}
for the remaining types of groups

Suppose
G
is a simple adjoint group of type
F_{4}
,
G_{2}
,
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
,
E_{8}
,
{{}^{3}\!}D_{4}
, or
{{}^{2}\!}E_{6}
. An inspection of the tables in
[
1
, Section 9]
shows that the map
\zeta
is injective. Since
G
is adjoint, we know
\chi_{G}
is bijective. Since
\zeta=\chi_{G}\circ\psi_{G}
, we conclude that
\psi_{G}
must be injective.

From Lemma
3.1.1
we conclude that
\psi_{G}
must be injective independent of the isogeny type of
G
. In particular,
\psi_{G}
is injective when
G
is an almost-simple group of type
F_{4}
,
G_{2}
,
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
,
E_{8}
,
{{}^{3}\!}D_{4}
, or
{{}^{2}\!}E_{6}
.

4.
Kac diagrams for
\vartheta
-elliptic conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}

In this section
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a simple complex Lie algebra and
G
is a semisimple complex group with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Suppose the pinned automorphism
\vartheta
has order
f
.

4.1.
Notation for Kac diagrams

We begin by recalling the setup of
[
20
]
for Kac diagrams.

Let
(\check{\omega}_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Delta}\subset V=\X_{*}(A)\otimes\mathbb{R}
be the fundamental coweights with respect to
\Delta
. That is,
\check{\omega}_{\alpha}(\beta)=\delta_{\alpha\beta}
for
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta
. Here
\delta_{\alpha\beta}=1
if
\alpha=\beta
and is zero otherwise.

For
\alpha\in\Phi=\Phi(G,A)
, define
\bar{\alpha}=\res_{V^{\vartheta}}\alpha
and set

\gamma_{\alpha}=\sum\bar{\beta}
where the sum is over those
\beta\in\Phi
such that
\bar{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\bar{\alpha}
. The set
\Phi_{\vartheta}:=\{\gamma_{\alpha}\,|\,\alpha\in\Phi\}
is the reduced root system for
G^{\vartheta}
with respect to
A^{\vartheta}
. The set
\Delta_{\vartheta}=\{\gamma_{\alpha}\,|\,\alpha\in\Delta\}
is a basis for
\Phi_{\vartheta}
with respect to
G^{\vartheta}
,
B^{\vartheta}
, and
A^{\vartheta}
. If
\vartheta
is nontrivial, then
\left|\Delta_{\vartheta}\right|<\left|\Delta\right|
.

The Weyl group
N_{G^{\vartheta}}(A)/A^{\vartheta}
is
\tilde{W}^{\vartheta}
, and
V^{\vartheta}
is the reflection representation for
\tilde{W}^{\vartheta}
. As before we have an affine simplicial decomposition of
V^{\vartheta}
, and the set of simple roots
\Delta_{\vartheta}
determines a unique alcove
C_{\vartheta}
that contains the origin of
V^{\vartheta}
. Note that
C_{\vartheta}\cap C^{\vartheta}\neq\emptyset
, and it can happen that
C_{\vartheta}
is not
C^{\vartheta}
.

4.1.1.
The constants
b_{\gamma}
and
c_{\gamma}

Let
\tilde{\gamma}_{0}
denote the highest root in
\Phi_{\vartheta}
with respect to
\Delta_{\vartheta}
and write
\tilde{\gamma}_{0}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Delta_{\vartheta}}c_{\gamma}\gamma
for
c_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
The constants
c_{\gamma}
may be found in
[
2
, Planche I – IX]
.

For
\gamma\in\Delta_{\vartheta}
choose
\alpha\in\Delta
such that
\gamma=\gamma_{\alpha}
. Let
\left|\gamma\right|_{\vartheta}
denote the size of the
\langle\vartheta\rangle
-orbit of
\alpha
and set

\check{\mu}_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{f}\sum_{j=0}^{f-1}\vartheta^{j}\check{\omega}_{\alpha}.

For
\gamma\in\Delta_{\vartheta}
we define
b_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
by requiring

\check{\mu}_{\gamma}(\rho)=\frac{b_{\gamma}f}{c_{\gamma}\left|\gamma\right|_{\vartheta}}\delta_{\gamma\rho}

for all
\rho\in\Delta_{\vartheta}
. If
\vartheta
is trivial, then
\Delta=\Delta_{\vartheta}
,
f=1
,
\left|\gamma\right|_{\vartheta}=1
, and
\check{\mu}_{\gamma}=\check{\omega}_{\gamma}
for all
\gamma\in\Delta_{\vartheta}=\Delta
. It follows that
b_{\gamma}=c_{\gamma}
for all
\gamma\in\Delta
. If
\vartheta
is not trivial, then the constants
b_{\gamma}
may be found in, for example,
[
20
, Table 2]
. Whenever we need to know the values of
b_{\gamma}
, we will provide them.

If we define
\gamma_{0}=1-\tilde{\gamma}_{0}
, then
\tilde{\Delta}_{\vartheta}=\Delta\cup\{\gamma_{0}\}
is a basis with respect to
C_{\vartheta}
for the affine roots determined by
G^{\vartheta}
and
A^{\vartheta}
. We set
c_{\gamma_{0}}=b_{\gamma_{0}}=1
.

4.1.2.
Kac diagrams

As discussed in the introduction, if
g\in G
has order
m
, then
g
determines a point, call it
x_{g}
, in the closure of
C_{\vartheta}
. A Kac diagram is a labeling of the affine Dynkin diagram that encodes the location of this point. We explain how this works.

Let
(v_{\gamma}\,|\,\gamma\in\tilde{\Delta}_{\vartheta})
denote the vertices of
C_{\vartheta}
. We let
v_{\gamma_{0}}
be the origin and then

v_{\gamma}=\dfrac{\left|\gamma\right|_{\vartheta}}{b_{\gamma}f}\cdot\check{\mu}_{\gamma}

for
\gamma\in\Delta_{\vartheta}
. Every element
x
in the closure of
C_{\vartheta}
may be written in barycentric coordinates as

x=\sum_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Delta}_{\vartheta}}x_{\gamma}v_{\gamma}

where
\sum x_{\gamma}=1
and
x_{\gamma}\geq 0
for all
\gamma
. From, for example
[
20
, Theorem 3.7]
, these conditions imply the existence of
s_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that

(4.1.1)

x_{g}=\frac{f}{m}\sum_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Delta}_{\vartheta}}s_{\gamma}b_{\gamma}\cdot v_{\gamma}=\frac{f}{m}\sum_{\gamma\in{\Delta}_{\vartheta}}s_{\gamma}b_{\gamma}\cdot v_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{\gamma\in{\Delta}_{\vartheta}}s_{\gamma}\left|\gamma\right|_{\vartheta}\cdot\check{\mu}_{\gamma}.

We will also have

(4.1.2)

m=f\sum_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Delta}_{\vartheta}}s_{\gamma}b_{\gamma}.

The list
(s_{\gamma}\,|\,\gamma\in\tilde{\Delta}_{\vartheta})
determines a Kac diagram attached to
g
as follows. First, remove any factors that are common to all of the
s_{\gamma}
so that we may assume that the
s_{\gamma}
are relatively prime. Then label the node in the affine Dynkin diagram corresponding to
\gamma\in\tilde{\Delta}_{\vartheta}
with the corresponding non-negative integer
s_{\gamma}
.

Remark 4.1.3
.

Suppose we are given a Kac diagram for
G^{\vartheta}
with labels
(s_{\gamma}\,|\,\gamma\in\tilde{\Delta}_{\vartheta})
. If we
define

m
using Equation
4.1.2
and these
s_{\gamma}
, then Equation
4.1.1
determines a point in
C_{\vartheta}
.

Remark 4.1.4
.

If
G
is adjoint, then the
s_{\gamma}
are automatically relatively prime and constructing an element
g
from a given Kac diagram is straightforward. However, if
G
is not adjoint, then the condition that the labels of the Kac diagram be relatively prime is often too restrictive – there may not even be an element of order
m=f\sum s_{\gamma}b_{\gamma}
in
G
. In this case one can first construct an element in the adjoint form of
G
and then lift this element into
G
.

Remark 4.1.5
.

In this paper we restrict our attention to finite order elements of
G
that normalize
A
and have
\vartheta
-elliptic image in
\tilde{W}
.
Let
n
be such an element, and assume it has order
m
. Let
\xi
be a primitive
m^{th}
root of unity. In this case, there exists
\lambda\in\X_{*}(A^{\vartheta})
such that
n
is
\vartheta
-conjugate to
\lambda(\xi)
and the point
x_{n}\in C_{\vartheta}
is equal to
\lambda/m
(see, for example,
[
5
, Lemma 3.5.1]
). From Equation (
4.1.1
) we must have
\lambda=\sum_{\gamma\in{\Delta}_{\vartheta}}s_{\gamma}\left|\gamma\right|_{\vartheta}\cdot\check{\mu}_{\gamma}
with
s_{\gamma}\left|\gamma\right|_{\vartheta}\geq 0
. In terms of the explicit realizations of
G
we have adopted, this means that this
\lambda
must look like

\lambda(t)=\Diag(t^{a_{1}},t^{a_{2}},\ldots)

for
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\cdots
.

Remark
4.1.5
will be used repeatedly in Sections
4.2
–
4.7
.

4.2.
How to create the Kac diagram for the elliptic conjugacy class in
\tilde{W}
for
A_{\ell-1}
with
\ell\geq 2

We adopt the notation of Section
3.2
.
As discussed earlier, there is only one elliptic conjugacy class in the Weyl group. We will show how to construct the Kac diagram associated to this elliptic conjugacy class. We adopt the notation of Section
3.2
.

Since
\vartheta
is trivial, we have
f=1
. We take the simple roots
\gamma
in
\Delta_{\vartheta}=\Delta
to be the roots
\gamma_{k}=\alpha_{k}
for
1\leq k<\ell
. For coweights we take
\check{\mu}_{i}=e_{1}+e_{2}+\cdots+e_{i}-\frac{i}{\ell}(e_{1}+e_{2}+\cdots+e_{\ell})
for
1\leq i<\ell
.

The affine Dynkin diagram is

\gamma_{\mathrlap{0}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{1}}

\Longleftrightarrow

for
\ell=2
, and it is

\gamma_{\mathrlap{1}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{2}}

…
\gamma_{\mathrlap{\ell-2}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{\ell-1}}

\gamma_{0}

for
\ell>2
. We have
b_{k}=c_{k}=1
for
0\leq k<\ell
, so the
b_{\gamma}
are given by the diagram

{1}

{1}

\Longleftrightarrow

for
\ell=2
, and by the diagram

{1}

{1}

…
{1}

{1}

1

for
\ell>2
.

The elliptic-conjugacy class in
\tilde{W}
corresponds to the partition
(\ell)
of
\ell
, and we constructed a representative
n_{(\ell)}
for this conjugacy class in Section
3.2
.
The characteristic polynomial of
n_{({\ell})}
with respect to its natural action on
\mathbb{C}^{\ell}
is

q_{n_{(\ell)}}(t)=t^{\ell}+(-1)^{\ell}
. We need to consider two cases.

4.2.1.
\ell
is even

Since
\ell
is even we have
q_{n_{(\ell)}}(t)=t^{\ell}+1
.
Let
m=2\ell
and let
\xi
be a primitive
m^{\text{th}}
root of unity. We have

q_{n_{(\ell)}}(t)=\prod_{a=1}^{\ell/2}(t-\xi^{2a-1})(t-\xi^{-(2a-1)}).

Guided by Remark
4.1.5
we create the length
\ell
decreasing list
(\ell-1,\ell-3,\ldots,3,1,-1,-3,\ldots,-(\ell-1))
. Denote by
\sigma_{i}
the
i^{\text{th}}
item in this list to obtain a decreasing list
(\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\sigma_{3}\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{\ell-1}\geq\sigma_{\ell})
. The element
d_{(\ell)}=\Diag(\xi^{\sigma_{1}},\xi^{\sigma_{2}},\ldots,\xi^{\sigma_{\ell-1}},\xi^{\sigma_{\ell}})
in
\SL_{\ell}
has characteristic polynomial
q_{n_{(\ell)}}
for its standard action on
\mathbb{C}^{\ell}
.

Since the linear factors
(t-\xi^{\sigma_{j}})
for
1\leq j\leq\ell
must occur in
q_{n_{(\ell)}}
, we conclude that, up to the action of
\tilde{W}
,
d_{(\ell)}
is the unique element of
A
that is
G
-conjugate to
n_{(\ell)}
.

We can now read off the Kac diagram for
n_{(\ell)}
from
d_{(\ell)}
.
Note that
d_{(\ell)}=\lambda_{({\ell})}(\xi)
where

\lambda_{({\ell})}=2\check{\mu}_{1}+2\check{\mu}_{2}+\ldots+2\check{\mu}_{\ell-1}.

Since
\left|\gamma\right|_{\vartheta}
is
1
for all
\gamma\in\Delta_{\vartheta}
and

\lambda_{({\ell})}/m=\frac{1}{m}[2\check{\mu}_{1}+2\check{\mu}_{2}+\ldots+2\check{\mu}_{\ell-1}],

from Equation
4.1.1
we conclude that the
s_{\gamma}
are all
2
for
\gamma\in\Delta
. In order to satisfy Equation
4.1.2
we must also have
s_{\gamma_{0}}=2
. Dividing all terms by
2
, we have:

Lemma 4.2.1
.

The Kac diagram for the elliptic conjugacy class in a group of type
A_{\ell-1}
with
\ell\geq 2
even is the same as the diagram for
b_{\gamma}
above.

.

and let

Guided by Remark

we create the length

decreasing list
((\ell-1)/2,(\ell-3)/2,\ldots,1,0,-1,\ldots,-(\ell-1)/2)
. Denote the

\sigma_{i}
to obtain a decreasing list

for its standard action on
\mathbb{C}^{\ell}
.

(t-\xi^{\sigma_{j}})
for

must occur in

, we conclude that, up to the action of

,
d_{(\ell)}
is the unique element of

that is

.

We can now read off the Kac diagram for

from

.

where

\lambda_{({\ell})}=\check{\mu}_{1}+\check{\mu}_{2}+\ldots+\check{\mu}_{\ell-1}.

Since
\left|\gamma\right|_{\vartheta}
is
1
for all
\gamma\in\Delta_{\vartheta}
and

\lambda_{({\ell})}/m=\frac{1}{m}[\check{\mu}_{1}+\check{\mu}_{2}+\ldots+\check{\mu}_{\ell-1}],

from Equation
4.1.1
we conclude that the
s_{\gamma}
are all

for
\gamma\in\Delta
. In order to satisfy Equation
4.1.2
we must also have
s_{\gamma_{0}}=1
. Thus, we have:

.

The Kac diagram for the elliptic conjugacy class in a group of type
A_{\ell-1}
with
\ell\geq 3
odd is the same as the diagram for

above.

How to create a Kac diagram for

. We take the simple roots

\gamma_{k}=\alpha_{k}

. For coweights we take
\check{\mu}_{i}=e_{1}+e_{2}+\cdots+e_{i}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{1}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{2}}

{2}

{2}

{2}

A Kac diagram describes a diagonal matrix in
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}
that is
G
-conjugate to
n_{\vec{\ell}}
. Thus, we want to find a diagonal matrix in
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}
such that its characteristic polynomial for the standard action on
\mathbb{C}^{2\ell+1}
is

q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}(t)=(t-(-1)^{\mu})\cdot\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t^{2\ell_{\nu}}-1).

Let
m=2\lcm(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{\mu})
and let
\xi
be a primitive
m^{\text{th}}
root of unity. We have

q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}(t)=(t-(-1)^{\mu})\cdot\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t^{2\ell_{\nu}}-1)=(t-(-1)^{\mu})\cdot\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t-1)(t+1)\prod_{a_{\nu}=1}^{\ell_{\nu}-1}(t-\xi^{ma_{\nu}/2\ell_{\nu}})(t-\xi^{-ma_{\nu}/2\ell_{\nu}}).

Guided by Remark
4.1.5
we create a length
\ell
decreasing list as follows: order the positive integers
ma_{\nu}/2\ell_{\nu}
in decreasing order, then pre-append
\lceil\mu/2\rceil
copies of
m/2
and post-append
\lfloor\mu/2\rfloor
zeroes. We thus obtain a list
(\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\sigma_{3}\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{\ell-1}\geq\sigma_{\ell})
. The element
d_{\vec{\ell}}=\Diag(\xi^{\sigma_{1}},\xi^{\sigma_{2}},\ldots,\xi^{\sigma_{\ell-1}},\xi^{\sigma_{\ell}},1,\xi^{-\sigma_{\ell}},\xi^{-\sigma_{\ell-1}},\ldots,\xi^{-\sigma_{2}},\xi^{-\sigma_{1}})
in
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell+1}
has characteristic polynomial
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}
for its standard action on
\mathbb{C}^{2\ell+1}
.
Since the linear factors
(t-(1)^{\mu})
along with
(t-\xi^{\sigma_{j}})
and
(t-\xi^{-\sigma_{j}})
for

must occur in

, we conclude that, up to the action of
\tilde{W}
,
d_{\vec{\ell}}
is the unique element of
A
that is

-conjugate to
n_{\vec{\ell}}
.

We can now read off the Kac diagram for
n_{\vec{\ell}}
from
d_{\vec{\ell}}
.
Note that
d_{\vec{\ell}}=\lambda_{\vec{\ell}}(\xi)
where

\lambda_{\vec{\ell}}=(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})\check{\mu}_{1}+(\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3})\check{\mu}_{2}+\cdots+(\sigma_{\ell-1}-\sigma_{\ell})\check{\mu}_{\ell-1}+\sigma_{\ell}\check{\mu}_{\ell}.

Since
\left|\gamma\right|_{\vartheta}
is
1
for all
\gamma\in\Delta_{\vartheta}
and

\lambda_{\vec{\ell}}/m=\frac{1}{m}[(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})\check{\mu}_{1}+(\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3})\check{\mu}_{2}+\cdots+(\sigma_{\ell-1}-\sigma_{\ell})\check{\mu}_{\ell-1}+\sigma_{\ell}\check{\mu}_{\ell}],

from Equation
4.1.1

and
s_{\gamma_{k}}=\sigma_{k}-\sigma_{k+1}
for
1\leq k\leq\ell-1
.
From Equation
4.1.2
we conclude that
s_{\gamma_{0}}=m-(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})
. Remove any factors that are common to all of the
s_{\gamma}
for
\gamma\in\tilde{\Delta}
and label the extended affine Dynkin diagram with the resulting
s_{\gamma}
.

We have proved:

Lemma 4.3.1
.

Fix a partition
\vec{\ell}=(\ell_{\mu},\ell_{\mu-1},\ldots,\ell_{2},\ell_{1})
of
\ell\geq 3
. Let
m=2\lcm(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{\mu})
. Append
\lceil\mu/2\rceil

and
\lfloor\mu/2\rfloor
copies of zero to the list

(a_{\nu}m/2\ell_{\nu}\,|\,\text{$1\leq\nu\leq\mu$ and $1\leq a_{\nu}\leq\ell_{\nu}-1$})

and then place the elements of the resulting list in decreasing order:

(\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\sigma_{3}\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{\ell-1}\geq\sigma_{\ell})
.
After removing any factors that are common to all of the labels, the Kac diagram for
n_{\vec{\ell}}

\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3}

) for the Kac diagrams appearing there are (from top to bottom):
(n)
,
(n/2,n/2)
for
n
even,
(n/k,n/k,n/k,\ldots,n/k,n/k)
for
k>2
even and
k
divides
n
, and
(n/k,n/k,n/k,\ldots,n/k,n/k)
for
k>1
odd and
k
divides
n
. The final two partitions in the previous sentence are identical and this correspondence between Kac diagrams and partitions agrees with
[
21
, Table 12]
.
From Remark
3.3.1
these partitions correspond to the regular elliptic elements in a Weyl group of type
B_{n}
.

4.4.
How to create a Kac diagram for
n_{\vec{\ell}}
for
C_{\ell}
with
\ell\geq 2

We adopt the notation of Section
3.4
. Fix a partition
\vec{\ell}
of
\ell
. We will show how to construct the Kac diagram for
n_{\vec{\ell}}
.

Since
\vartheta
is trivial, we have
f=1
. We take the simple roots
\gamma
in
\Delta_{\vartheta}=\Delta
to be the roots
\gamma_{k}=\alpha_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq\ell
.
For fundamental coweights with respect to our basis we take

\check{\mu}_{i}=e_{1}+e_{2}+\cdots+e_{i}
for
1\leq i<\ell
and
\check{\mu}_{\ell}=1/2(e_{1}+e_{2}+\cdots+e_{\ell})
.
The affine Dynkin diagram is

\gamma_{\mathrlap{0}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{1}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{2}}

…
\gamma_{\mathrlap{\ell-2}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{\ell-1}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{\ell}}

\Rightarrow
\Leftarrow

We have
b_{\gamma_{k}}=c_{\gamma_{k}}
for
0\leq k<\ell
, so the
b_{\gamma}
are given by the diagram

{1}

{2}

{2}

…
{2}

{2}

{1}

\Rightarrow
\Leftarrow

A Kac diagram describes a diagonal matrix in
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2\ell}
that is
G
-conjugate to
n_{\vec{\ell}}
. Thus, we want to find a diagonal matrix in
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2\ell}
such that its characteristic polynomial for the standard action on
\mathbb{C}^{2\ell}
is

q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}(t)=\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t^{2\ell_{\nu}}+1).

Let
m=4\lcm(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{\mu})
; this is the order of
n_{\vec{\ell}}
.
Let
\xi
be a primitive
m^{\text{th}}
root of unity. We have

q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}(t)=\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}\prod_{a_{\nu}=1}^{\ell_{\nu}}(t-\xi^{(2a_{\nu}-1)m/4\ell_{\nu})})(t-\xi^{-(2a_{\nu}-1)m/4\ell_{\nu})}).

Guided by Remark
4.1.5
we create a length
\ell
decreasing list as follows: order the positive integers
(2a_{\nu}-1)m/4\ell_{\nu}
for
1\leq\nu\leq\mu
and
1\leq\vec{a}_{\nu}\leq\ell_{\nu}
in decreasing order. We thus obtain a list
S=(\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\sigma_{3}\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{\ell-1}\geq\sigma_{\ell})
.

The element
d_{\vec{\ell}}=\Diag(\xi^{\sigma_{1}},\xi^{\sigma_{2}},\ldots,\xi^{\sigma_{\ell-1}},\xi^{\sigma_{\ell}},\xi^{-\sigma_{\ell}},\xi^{-\sigma_{\ell-1}},\ldots,\xi^{-\sigma_{2}},\xi^{-\sigma_{1}})
in
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2\ell}
has characteristic polynomial
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}
for its standard action on
\mathbb{C}^{2\ell}
.
Since the linear factors
(t-\xi^{\sigma_{j}})
and
(t-\xi^{-\sigma_{j}})
for
1\leq j\leq\ell
must occur in
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}
, we conclude that, up to the action of
\tilde{W}
,
d_{\vec{\ell}}
is the unique element of
A
that is
G
-conjugate to
n_{\vec{\ell}}
.

We can now read off the Kac diagram for
n_{\vec{\ell}}
from
d_{\vec{\ell}}
.
Note that
d_{\vec{\ell}}=\lambda_{\vec{\ell}}(\xi)
where

\lambda_{\vec{\ell}}=(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})\check{\mu}_{1}+(\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3})\check{\mu}_{2}+\ldots+(\sigma_{\ell-1}-\sigma_{\ell})\check{\mu}_{\ell-1}+2\sigma_{\ell}\check{\mu}_{\ell}.

Since
\left|\gamma\right|_{\vartheta}
is
1
for all
\gamma\in\Delta_{\vartheta}
and

\lambda_{\vec{\ell}}/m=\frac{1}{m}[(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})\check{\mu}_{1}+(\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3})\check{\mu}_{2}+\ldots+(\sigma_{\ell-1}-\sigma_{\ell})\check{\mu}_{\ell-1}+2\sigma_{\ell}\check{\mu}_{\ell}],

from Equation
4.1.1
we conclude that

s_{\gamma_{\ell}}=2\sigma_{\ell}
and
s_{\gamma_{k}}=(\sigma_{k}-\sigma_{k+1})
for
1\leq k\leq\ell-1
.
The coefficient
s_{\gamma_{0}}=m-2\sigma_{1}
is derived using Equation
4.1.2
.
Remove any factors that are common to all of the
s_{\gamma}
for
\gamma\in\tilde{\Delta}
and label the extended affine Dynkin diagram with the resulting
s_{\gamma}
.

We have proved:

Lemma 4.4.1
.

Fix a partition
\vec{\ell}=(\ell_{\mu},\ell_{\mu-1},\ldots,\ell_{2},\ell_{1})
of
\ell\geq 2
. Let
m=4\lcm(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{\mu})
. Take the list

((2a_{\nu}-1)m/4\ell_{\nu}\,|\,\text{$1\leq\nu\leq\mu$ and $1\leq a_{\nu}\leq\ell_{\nu}$})

and place the elements in decreasing order:

(\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\sigma_{3}\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{\ell-1}\geq\sigma_{\ell})
.
After removing any factors that are common to all of the labels, the Kac diagram for
n_{\vec{\ell}}
in a group of type
C_{\ell}
with
\ell\geq 2
is given by

m-2\sigma_{1}

\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}

\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3}

…

\sigma_{\ell-2}-\sigma_{\ell-1}

\sigma_{\ell-1}-\sigma_{\ell}

{2\sigma_{\ell}}

\Rightarrow
\Leftarrow

∎

4.5.
How to create a Kac diagram for
n_{\vec{\ell}}
for
D_{\ell}
with
\ell\geq 4

We adopt the notation of Section
3.5
. Fix a partition
\vec{\ell}
of
\ell
with an even number of parts. We will show how to construct the Kac diagram for
n_{\vec{\ell}}
.

Since
\vartheta
is trivial, we have
f=1
. We take the simple roots
\gamma
in
\Delta_{\vartheta}=\Delta
to be the roots
\gamma_{k}=\alpha_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq\ell
.
For fundamental coweights with respect to our basis we take

\check{\mu}_{i}=e_{1}+e_{2}+\cdots+e_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq\ell-2
,
\check{\mu}_{\ell-1}=1/2(e_{1}+e_{2}+\cdots e_{\ell-2}+e_{\ell-1}-e_{\ell})
, and
\check{\mu}_{\ell}=1/2(e_{1}+e_{2}+\cdots e_{\ell-2}+e_{\ell-1}+e_{\ell})
.
The affine Dynkin diagram is

\gamma_{\mathrlap{1}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{2}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{3}}

…
\gamma_{\mathrlap{\ell-3}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{\ell-2}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{\ell-1}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{0}}

\gamma_{\mathrlap{\ell}}

We have
b_{\gamma_{k}}=c_{\gamma_{k}}
for
0\leq k<\ell
, so the
b_{\gamma}
are given by the diagram

{1}

{2}

{2}

…
{2}

{2}

{1}

{1}

{1}

A Kac diagram describes a diagonal matrix in
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell}
that is
G
-conjugate to
n_{\vec{\ell}}
. Thus, we want to find a diagonal matrix in
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell}
such that its characteristic polynomial for the standard action on
\mathbb{C}^{2\ell}
is

q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}(t)=\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t^{2\ell_{\nu}}-1).

Let
m=2\lcm(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{\mu})
and let
\xi
be a primitive
m^{\text{th}}
root of unity. We have

q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}(t)=\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t^{2\ell_{\nu}}-1)=\prod_{\nu=1}^{\mu}(t-1)(t+1)\prod_{a_{\nu}=1}^{\ell_{\nu}-1}(t-\xi^{ma_{\nu}/2\ell_{\nu}})(t-\xi^{-ma_{\nu}/2\ell_{\nu}}).

Guided by Remark
4.1.5
we create a length
\ell
decreasing list as follows: order the positive integers
ma_{\nu}/2\ell_{\nu}
in decreasing order, then pre-append
\mu/2
copies of
m/2
and post-append
\mu/2
zeroes. We thus obtain a list
(\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\sigma_{3}\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{\ell-1}\geq\sigma_{\ell})
. The element
d_{\vec{\ell}}=\Diag(\xi^{\sigma_{1}},\xi^{\sigma_{2}},\ldots,\xi^{\sigma_{\ell-1}},\xi^{\sigma_{\ell}},\xi^{-\sigma_{\ell}},\xi^{-\sigma_{\ell-1}},\ldots,\xi^{-\sigma_{2}},\xi^{-\sigma_{1}})
in
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2\ell}
has characteristic polynomial
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}
for its standard action on
\mathbb{C}^{2\ell}
.

Since the linear factors
(t-\xi^{\sigma_{j}})
and
(t-\xi^{-\sigma_{j}})
for
1\leq j\leq\ell
must occur in
q_{n_{\vec{\ell}}}
, we conclude that, up to the action of
\tilde{W}
,
d_{\vec{\ell}}
is the unique element of
A
that is
G
-conjugate to
n_{\vec{\ell}}
.

We can now read off the Kac diagram for
n_{\vec{\ell}}
from
d_{\vec{\ell}}
.
Note that
d_{\vec{\ell}}=\lambda_{\vec{\ell}}(\xi)
where

\lambda_{\vec{\ell}}=({\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}})\check{\mu}_{1}+({\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3}})\check{\mu}_{2}+\ldots+({\sigma_{\ell-2}-\sigma_{\ell-1}})\check{\mu}_{\ell-2}+(\sigma_{\ell-1}-\sigma_{\ell})\check{\mu}_{\ell-1}+(\sigma_{\ell-1}+\sigma_{\ell})\check{\mu}_{\ell}.

Since
\left|\gamma\right|_{\vartheta}
is
1
for all
\gamma\in\Delta_{\vartheta}
and

\lambda_{\vec{\ell}}/m=\frac{1}{m}[({\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}})\check{\mu}_{1}+({\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3}})\check{\mu}_{2}+\ldots+({\sigma_{\ell-2}-\sigma_{\ell-1}})\check{\mu}_{\ell-2}+(\sigma_{\ell-1}-\sigma_{\ell})\check{\mu}_{\ell-1}+(\sigma_{\ell-1}+\sigma_{\ell})\check{\mu}_{\ell}],

from Equation
4.1.1
we conclude that

s_{\gamma_{\ell}}=\sigma_{\ell-1}+\sigma_{\ell}
and

s_{\gamma_{k}}=(\sigma_{k}-\sigma_{k+1})
for
1\leq k\leq\ell-1
.
The coefficient
s_{\gamma_{0}}=m-(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})
is derived using Equation
4.1.2
.
Remove any factors that are common to all of the
s_{\gamma}
for
\gamma\in\tilde{\Delta}
and label the extended affine Dynkin diagram with the resulting
s_{\gamma}
.

We have proved:

Lemma 4.5.1
.

Fix a partition
\vec{\ell}=(\ell_{\mu},\ell_{\mu-1},\ldots,\ell_{2},\ell_{1})
of
\ell\geq 4
with
\mu
even. Let
m=2\lcm(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{\mu})
. Append
\mu/2
copies of
m/2
and
\mu/2
copies of zero to the list

(a_{\nu}m/2\ell_{\nu}\,|\,\text{$1\leq\nu\leq\mu$ and $1\leq a_{\nu}\leq\ell_{\nu}-1$})

and then place the elements of the resulting list in decreasing order:

(\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\sigma_{3}\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{\ell-1}\geq\sigma_{\ell})
.
After removing any factors that are common to all of the labels, the Kac diagram for
n_{\vec{\ell}}
in a group of type
D_{\ell}
with
\ell\geq 4
is given by

\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3}

\sigma_{3}-\sigma_{4}

\sigma_{4}-\sigma_{5}

…

\sigma_{\ell-4}-\sigma_{\ell-3}

\sigma_{\ell-3}-\sigma_{\ell-2}

\sigma_{\ell-2}-\sigma_{\ell-1}

{m-(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})}

{\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}}

{\sigma_{\ell-1}+\sigma_{\ell}}

{\sigma_{\ell-1}-\sigma_{\ell}}

∎

.

. For

, and our results agree with the results found there.
They also agree with those found in the table in

, Section A.5]
; the partitions (in the notation of
[

, Section A.5]
) for the Kac diagrams appearing there are (from top to bottom):
(n-1,1)
,

for
